# *Maebashi Witches* Episode 6

## 「チョコのまんまるチョコケーキ」

### “Choco’s Round Chocolate Cake”

The official synopsis presents Episode 6 as the point where the shop has begun operating smoothly, Kyouka becomes concerned about Choco’s apparent lack of motivation, and Yuina plans Choco’s birthday party. [![](https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?domain=https://www.maebashi-witches.com&sz=128)オリジナルアニメ「前橋ウィッチーズ」公式サイト](https://www.maebashi-witches.com/story/)

## Bundle assessment

The Episode 6 bundle is complete:

-   23:41 runtime

-   519 Japanese subtitle cues

-   686 retained frames after visual deduplication

-   35 contact sheets

-   complete cold-open performance, birthday song, ending, post-credits scene, and next-episode preview


This is a transitional episode rather than a completed customer arc. It consolidates the ensemble after the emotionally severe Mai–Yua story, reveals Kyouka’s private motives, and quietly prepares Choco’s conflict.

Its lighter tone should not be mistaken for narrative filler. Episode 6 asks:

> **How well do the witches actually know one another, and how much of their “care” is based on assumptions about the role each girl performs?**

---

# Episode summary

## The shop becomes competent

The episode opens with the witches completing another successful performance for a customer named Kasumi.

The song’s message is aggressively positive:

> 「ミスしたって取り戻せばいーじゃんね」
> “Even if you make a mistake, you can just recover from it.”

> 「山あり谷ありで
> 凸凹してたって
> かすり傷さ」
> “There are mountains and valleys; even if things are uneven, it’s only a scratch.”

After Kasumi’s heart-flower blooms, Kyouka immediately invites the next customer to speak.

The witches now operate like a functioning service team. They can handle several customers in one day and no longer collapse after every song.

## Heat, Mapo, and the air conditioner

The girls cool their feet in a small pool inside the flower shop. Yuina celebrates their improved magical stamina, while Azu complains about the heat and demands an air conditioner.

Keroppe says installing one will consume Mapo.

The girls have finally accumulated more than one thousand points, so spending some of them feels dangerous. Kyouka argues that this is a legitimate workplace investment:

-   customer traffic is increasing;

-   the Tanabata festival is approaching;

-   summer vacation will allow longer opening hours;

-   and preserving the workers’ health will allow them to earn more points later.


The group agrees.

Unlike Mai’s secret spending, this use of Mapo is:

-   openly discussed;

-   collectively authorized;

-   directed toward shared working conditions;

-   and justified in relation to both well-being and future productivity.


## The group praises Kyouka

The others notice that Kyouka has become the shop’s practical leader.

She:

-   persuades everyone;

-   interviews customers effectively;

-   learns their problems without purchasing information from Keroppe;

-   organizes their labor;

-   and prevents wasteful Mapo spending.


They call her intelligent, rich, refined, and naturally competent.

Kyouka rejects the characterization:

> 「お嬢様でも天才でもない」
> 「普通だから」
> “I’m neither a rich young lady nor a genius. I’m ordinary.”

The insistence is more emotionally meaningful than factually accurate. Her family clearly possesses considerable wealth and status. What Kyouka resists is being turned into an exceptional social object.

## Kyouka watches Choco

Yuina notices that Kyouka has been observing Choco.

Kyouka explains that Choco has recently seemed:

-   sleepy;

-   frequently late;

-   insufficiently motivated;

-   and less committed to the shop than the others.


Yuina completely misreads Kyouka’s concern. Because Choco’s birthday is approaching, Yuina assumes Kyouka must be thinking about how to celebrate it.

When Yuina asks why Kyouka wants to become a witch, Kyouka first gives an admirable answer:

> 「誰かの役に立つ仕事に就きたい」
> “I want work through which I can help someone.”

> 「人の願いもかなえていけば
> ロクでもない世界も 少しは変わるかなって」
> “If I keep granting people’s wishes, maybe this terrible world will improve slightly.”

Yuina treats this as proof of Kyouka’s superior intelligence and moral seriousness.

Kyouka then provides the less polished truth.

## Kyouka wants to escape home

Kyouka says she wants to leave home as soon as possible.

She is tired of expectations concerning:

-   educational pedigree;

-   femininity and masculinity;

-   being her parents’ impressive daughter;

-   and living according to roles she considers meaningless.


She wants to live alone and do what she likes.

Her desire to help people is genuine, but she wonders whether socially useful work is also an **免罪符**, an absolution or protective license:

> If she is useful enough, perhaps society will permit her to live freely without condemning her.

This is one of the most self-aware confessions in the series so far. Kyouka recognizes that altruism and self-interest coexist inside the same aspiration.

## Mogutan

The thing Kyouka privately loves is a VTuber called Mogutan.

When asked whether Mogutan is male or female, Kyouka answers:

> 「モグタンはモグタンだよ」
> “Mogutan is Mogutan.”

Mogutan tells viewers:

> 「モグタンといるときは
> 本当の自分になって
> のんびりしてってね」
> “When you’re with Mogutan, become your true self and relax.”

Kyouka says that listening allows her to forget school and home. She occasionally sends small tips under the name “Kyon-chan,” which Mogutan acknowledges during streams.

Yuina concludes that Kyouka’s ideal future is helping people professionally while spending her private time immersed in Mogutan.

Kyouka agrees.

## Yuina has no future objective

Kyouka asks what Yuina wants to do after becoming a full witch.

Yuina has no answer.

She wants to:

-   sing with everyone;

-   talk;

-   grant customers’ wishes;

-   eat manju;

-   enjoy the present;

-   and eventually photograph her ultimate emotional moment.


But she does not possess a post-witchhood plan.

Kyouka calls her honest.

Yuina then decides that an extravagant birthday party will restore Choco’s motivation, strengthen the group, help everyone earn Mapo, advance their dreams, and produce an excellent photograph.

## Planning the party

The four girls organize the party through a video call while Choco is absent.

Their discussion gradually reveals how little they know about her.

They describe Choco as someone who:

-   says “Choco-Choco” constantly;

-   eats snacks;

-   sleeps;

-   follows other people’s lead;

-   arrives late;

-   leaves practical work to Kyouka;

-   and can be surprisingly shameless.


They become so irritated while listing these traits that someone jokingly suggests canceling the party.

Nevertheless, each contributes:

-   Kyouka asks her pastry-chef aunt to provide a professional cake and brings luxury snacks and drinks from home.

-   Azu handles the present because she considers herself the most stylish.

-   Mai helps coordinate the practical details.

-   Yuina plans decorations, a homemade “JK cake,” an original song, and the eventual photograph.


The group avoids using Mapo. The celebration will be produced through personal money, labor, access, and creativity.

## Mai continues Kumai

During the planning call, Yuina learns that Mai has resumed control of the Kumai account and is considering game streaming.

Mai says the account began because of Yua, but she has also made friends through it.

This is a quiet but major development.

Kumai no longer exists solely as an offering to Yua. Mai may now integrate her childhood interest in games, her fashion expertise, and the relationships she independently formed into a public identity of her own.

## Kyouka’s family

After the call, Kyouka’s mother enters their dim, spacious home carrying a fashionable gift received through a business contact.

She says Kyouka’s father was pleased by her test results:

> 「さすが俺の子だ」
> “As expected of my daughter.”

Kyouka may receive a recommendation to the university her parents attended. Her mother says she will surely like it because of the quality of its teaching and students.

Her father also wants to take her to dinner. Kyouka is instructed to wear the dress he bought.

Her mother then suggests that Kyouka grow her hair:

> 「せっかくママに似て キレイなんだから
> もっと武器は生かさなきゃ」
> “You’re beautiful like me, so you should make better use of that weapon.”

Kyouka responds politely and without open resistance.

She then retreats to Mogutan.

## Choco asks for a birthday cake

On the day of the party, Choco encounters Kyouka outside the shop and announces that it is her birthday.

She asks whether they can spend a little Mapo to create a cake.

When Kyouka resists, Choco points out that others have used points privately:

-   Azu removed a pimple.

-   Mai repaired a vase she broke.


Choco is more observant than Kyouka assumes.

Kyouka, unable to preserve the surprise, tells her to read the situation and go away temporarily.

## The birthday party

Choco enters the decorated shop. She is not sufficiently surprised for Yuina’s satisfaction and quickly asks whether there is cake.

The group changes its planned order and performs Yuina’s original birthday song.

Unlike the polished magical performances offered to customers, this sequence resembles a homemade recording:

-   camcorder framing;

-   rough, playful graphics;

-   direct address;

-   amateur lyrics;

-   improvised enthusiasm;

-   and the friends visibly performing for one another.


The two cakes are then revealed.

Choco initially seems merely hungry. When she sees them clearly, however, she begins crying:

> 「まんまるチョコケーキだ」
> “It’s a round chocolate cake.”

> 「チョコだけのまんまるケーキ
> 2つもある」
> “There are two whole round cakes just for Choco.”

Yuina cries because Choco is happy.

After blowing out the candles, Choco asks the group to say it once more:

> 「お誕生日 おめでとう」
> “Happy birthday.”

Choco answers:

> 「ありがとう」
> “Thank you.”

## Post-credits

The girls prepare to take a group photograph.

When Yuina asks Keroppe to take it, he complains that he would then be excluded. They decide to take a selfie so everyone can appear together.

Before the photograph can settle the episode into a perfect commemorative image, another customer arrives.

The timing visibly frustrates Choco.

---

# The core thesis

Episode 6 is about the difference between **performing a useful role** and being known as a person.

Kyouka is known as:

-   the intelligent one;

-   the responsible one;

-   the rich one;

-   the mediator;

-   the organizer;

-   the good daughter;

-   and the girl who helps everyone.


Choco is known as:

-   the cheerful one;

-   the lazy one;

-   the snack-loving one;

-   the cute mood-maker;

-   and the girl who appears to have no problems.


Both are being understood through functions.

The episode begins exposing the private selves behind those roles:

> Kyouka’s competence is partly a strategy for earning permission to escape.

> Choco’s cheerfulness does not mean she has no unmet emotional needs.

---

# Kyouka’s altruism is sincere—but also defensive

Kyouka’s confession is sophisticated because the episode refuses to choose between two reductive interpretations:

-   Kyouka wants to help people because she is morally admirable.

-   Kyouka only wants to look admirable so she can escape her family.


Both are incomplete.

She genuinely wants useful work. She also understands that usefulness can protect her autonomy.

Her reasoning resembles a social bargain:

> If I make myself valuable enough to others, they will lose the right to criticize how I live.

That is not true freedom. Her independence would still depend upon continued performance.

Kyouka wants to escape the identity of “proud daughter” by becoming an unimpeachably useful adult. She risks replacing one demanding role with another.

This connects directly to the flower shop. She has become indispensable there:

-   she extracts customers’ stories;

-   manages resources;

-   mediates conflict;

-   and keeps the group functioning.


The others’ praise is affectionate, but it also reproduces the structure she wants to escape. They rapidly turn her into “the exceptional competent one.”

Her response—

> 「普通だから」
> “I’m ordinary.”

—is a demand not to be converted into another achievement.

---

# The family home

Kyouka’s family does not appear overtly abusive.

Her parents:

-   praise her;

-   buy her gifts and clothes;

-   plan dinners;

-   care about her education;

-   and take pride in her beauty and academic success.


The problem is that nearly every kindness contains a script.

The test result belongs to her father:

> “As expected of **my** daughter.”

Her university path should reproduce her parents’ path.

The dress her father selected should be worn for the family dinner.

Her hair should grow because femininity and inherited beauty are assets she ought to exploit.

Kyouka is loved as an extension of the family’s successful image.

This is why her home is visually important. It is expensive but emotionally cold:

-   wide spaces;

-   subdued lighting;

-   formal distance;

-   controlled presentation;

-   and little sense that Kyouka has authored the environment.


Her material privilege is genuine. So is her lack of freedom.

The series does not equate wealth with emotional security.

---

# Mogutan and freedom from classification

Mogutan becomes meaningful immediately after Kyouka names **男らしさ** and **女らしさ** among the social constraints she hates.

When asked Mogutan’s gender, she refuses the question:

> Mogutan is Mogutan.

This need not mean that Mogutan is canonically nonbinary. More importantly, the persona creates a space where gender classification is irrelevant to participation.

Mogutan’s invitation is:

> Become your real self here and rest.

That is precisely what Kyouka cannot do at home.

Her private fandom therefore serves several functions:

-   escape from family expectations;

-   freedom from the competent-leader role;

-   a relationship conducted under her chosen nickname;

-   relief from binary gendered judgment;

-   and permission to consume something purposelessly because she likes it.


Kyouka does not have to help Mogutan.

She does not have to be impressive.

She can simply watch.

---

# Kyouka and Choco as emerging mirrors

Kyouka initially interprets Choco’s sleepiness as insufficient commitment.

That judgment is understandable. Kyouka carries a disproportionate share of the group’s organizational work, and Choco often appears to drift along while others compensate.

But Kyouka’s response may also contain projection.

Kyouka has learned that legitimacy requires:

-   competence;

-   discipline;

-   usefulness;

-   foresight;

-   and suppression of private fatigue.


Choco appears to violate every part of that system.

She:

-   sleeps publicly;

-   asks for things;

-   eats freely;

-   arrives late;

-   allows others to care for her;

-   and turns her identity into a playful performance.


Choco may therefore represent a form of ease Kyouka distrusts because she has never been permitted to possess it.

Conversely, Choco may experience Kyouka’s vigilance as intrusive management rather than care.

The conflict being prepared is not simply responsible girl versus lazy girl. It is likely to concern two incompatible survival strategies:

> Kyouka earns safety by being useful.

> Choco creates safety by keeping things light.

Both strategies conceal weakness.

---

# “Choco doesn’t seem to have any worries”

During the birthday planning, someone effectively says that Choco seems fortunate because she appears not to have any problems.

That line should be treated as a warning.

The show has repeatedly demonstrated that visible presentation does not reveal interior life:

-   Eiko’s academic success concealed uncertainty.

-   Rinko’s confidence concealed resignation.

-   Azu’s polished appearance concealed self-loathing.

-   Mai’s detachment concealed consuming attachment.

-   Yua’s casual confidence concealed status panic.

-   Kyouka’s competence conceals the desire to escape.


There is no reason Choco should be the exception.

Her exaggerated “Choco-chan” behavior may not be proof that she lacks depth. It may be the mechanism through which she prevents depth from becoming burdensome to others.

The cold-open song’s insistence that everything is merely a scratch may be part of that mechanism.

---

# The limits of positivity

The cold-open song says that mistakes can be corrected, tomorrow will shine, and everyone should remain positive.

That message fits the witches’ recent development. They have:

-   harmed customers;

-   stolen resources;

-   cheated;

-   exploited one another;

-   and nevertheless continued together.


But the phrasing also risks minimizing pain:

> “It’s only a scratch.”

Some experiences in the show have clearly not been minor scratches. Rinko says that hurtful words never disappear. Mai and Yua reach a bitter rather than happy ending. Azu’s body trauma cannot be solved through positivity.

Choco’s highly positive public persona may eventually confront something that cannot be turned into a cute slogan.

Episode 6 has not yet revealed that conflict, but it places the warning directly at the beginning.

---

# The party reveals how little they know Choco

Yuina decides that a spectacular party will restore Choco’s motivation before anyone asks Choco what she is feeling.

This follows the series’ established pattern:

1.  Someone displays a visible behavior.

2.  The witches invent an explanation.

3.  They design an intervention around that explanation.

4.  The person’s actual interior remains unknown.


Their planning conversation is unintentionally revealing. They want to celebrate Choco, yet struggle to identify personal details beyond her character gimmicks.

They know her function in the ensemble better than they know her.

The party still succeeds, but that success should not be interpreted as proof that their assumptions were correct. Choco may be happy to receive affection while still carrying whatever caused Kyouka’s concern.

---

# Yuina’s mixed motives

Yuina’s birthday plan is compassionate, but she attaches many projected outcomes to it:

-   Choco will become motivated.

-   The shop will earn more Mapo.

-   Everyone’s dreams will advance.

-   The group will become closer.

-   Yuina will obtain an emotional photograph.


This resembles Kyouka’s mixed altruism.

Yuina genuinely wants Choco to be happy. She also wants Choco’s happiness to improve the team and contribute to her photography project.

The episode does not condemn this impurity. Very few acts of care have only one motive.

The ethical question is whether the recipient is allowed to respond in a way that does not fulfill the helper’s imagined outcome.

That remains unresolved.

---

# Choco notices the Mapo hypocrisy

Choco’s request for a magical birthday cake is important because it disproves the idea that she is oblivious.

She knows that:

-   Azu used Mapo for cosmetic convenience.

-   Mai used Mapo to repair something she broke.

-   private exceptions already exist.


Her argument is reasonable:

> If other members use communal magic for personal needs, why should a small birthday cake be forbidden?

Kyouka does not answer with a consistent resource policy. She tells Choco to read the room because real cakes are already waiting inside.

The comedy hides an institutional problem. The group has become more cohesive but still governs shared resources through informal favoritism and secrecy.

Choco’s willingness to ask openly may actually be healthier than the others’ concealed spending.

---

# Why the real cakes matter

The group could have produced a cake instantly with Mapo.

Instead, they create the party through ordinary effort:

-   budgeting;

-   shopping;

-   family connections;

-   decoration;

-   planning;

-   songwriting;

-   baking or assembly;

-   and selecting a present.


The cakes matter because they contain time and intention.

This continues the series’ distinction between:

-   magic that bypasses authorship;

-   and support that enables meaningful human action.


The party is not valuable because the cakes are objectively superior. One is professionally made, while Yuina’s “JK cake” is deliberately amateur.

Their combined presence says:

> Choco was important enough for several different forms of effort.

---

# “Two round cakes just for Choco”

Choco’s strongest reaction is not to luxury, sophistication, or spectacle.

It is to exclusivity:

> There are two complete cakes **just for Choco**.

That wording echoes the shop’s central promise:

> 「ここは あなただけのお花屋さん」
> “This is a flower shop just for you.”

Choco spends much of her time making herself available as a communal mood:

-   everyone’s Choco-chan;

-   the mascot-like girl;

-   the source of catchphrases;

-   the cheerful accompaniment to other people’s problems.


The cakes temporarily make her the sole recipient.

Her tears suggest that being individually centered has greater emotional weight than the others expected.

It would be premature to infer a specific history of neglected birthdays. What the episode clearly establishes is:

> Choco deeply values being given something whole, personal, and unmistakably hers.

Her request that everyone say “happy birthday” again reinforces this. She wants to remain inside the moment and hear its meaning explicitly repeated.

---

# The birthday song as a nonmagical performance

The visual language of Yuina’s song differs sharply from the wish-granting performances:

-   home-video framing rather than cosmic stage polish;

-   playful subtitles and graphics;

-   visible amateurism;

-   awkward sincerity;

-   and no pretense of universal wisdom.


It is not an ethical thesis delivered to a customer.

It is a gift made for one friend.

This broadens the meaning of performance in the series. Singing need not be:

-   magical labor;

-   customer service;

-   point production;

-   or therapeutic intervention.


It can also be socially useless in the best sense.

The song earns no obvious Mapo. It produces joy anyway.

That is particularly meaningful after Episode 5 established that the magical system compensates only authorized shop labor. Episode 6 shows the girls choosing to perform care without compensation.

---

# Mai’s quiet recovery

Mai’s decision to continue Kumai and possibly begin game streaming is one of the episode’s most important minor developments.

She is bringing together parts of herself previously kept separate:

-   the gamer child;

-   the fashion expert;

-   the anonymous creator;

-   and the person who formed friendships through the account.


In Episodes 4–5, Kumai was “for Yua.”

Now Mai admits that something independent grew from it.

This is the beginning of her becoming a protagonist in her own work rather than Yua’s NPC.

Her response remains characteristically understated. She does not announce a grand new dream. She simply continues.

That modest continuity is the healthy version of her seventy-point philosophy.

---

# The air conditioner and collective consent

The Mapo debate at the beginning provides a subtle contrast with Mai’s earlier theft.

The group does spend communal points. The show therefore does not argue that saving resources is always morally superior.

The difference is process:

-   Kyouka identifies a shared need.

-   She explains short- and long-term effects.

-   Everyone has an opportunity to object.

-   The expenditure benefits all workers.

-   The decision is recorded socially rather than concealed.


This is the first time the girls govern magical capital like a functional collective.

The air conditioner is mundane, but institutionally it may be one of their most mature achievements.

---

# The title

Unlike the accusatory and philosophical titles of earlier episodes, **“Choco’s Round Chocolate Cake”** names a specific object in childish, repetitive language.

That tonal change matters.

The title does not ask:

-   what is authentic;

-   what is morally correct;

-   whether seventy points are enough;

-   or who controls a person’s identity.


It preserves a small memory:

> Choco looked at two cakes, called them round chocolate cakes, and cried because they belonged to her.

The object becomes significant because someone loved it.

That is very close to Yuina’s photographic philosophy. Emotional value does not always arrive as a grand ideological revelation. Sometimes it is attached to a cake whose description sounds silly to everyone except the person receiving it.

---

# The interrupted group photograph

The final selfie would have perfectly completed Yuina’s plan:

-   Choco is happy.

-   Everyone is together.

-   Keroppe is included.

-   The party succeeded.

-   The group has become closer.

-   An emotional photograph can preserve it.


Then a customer arrives.

The interruption places two identities of the shop in conflict:

-   private refuge for the five girls;

-   service workplace for anyone who enters with a wish.


The customer does not know or owe respect to their personal celebration. From her perspective, the shop is open.

Choco’s irritation is therefore understandable, but it also threatens the ethical basis of their work. The witches cannot promise that the shop is “just for you” while resenting a customer for arriving at an inconvenient emotional moment.

That conflict is a fitting endpoint for an episode about the boundary between care as role and care as relationship.

---

# Character updates

## Kyouka

Kyouka’s competence is now revealed as both genuine strength and defensive adaptation.

She wants:

-   autonomy;

-   an ungendered private self;

-   freedom from family scripting;

-   useful work;

-   and permission to enjoy something without justifying it.


Her danger is that she may make usefulness the price of her own existence and then judge Choco for refusing the same bargain.

## Choco

Choco remains the least fully disclosed member.

Episode 6 establishes that she is:

-   more observant than she appears;

-   comfortable asking directly for pleasure;

-   deeply affected by personalized care;

-   willing to let others serve her;

-   and perhaps more emotionally hungry than her carefree persona suggests.


Her tears do not yet explain her sleepiness or behavior. The birthday does not resolve Kyouka’s concern.

## Yuina

Yuina increasingly recreates the flower shop’s emotional structure in ordinary friendship.

She identifies someone who seems unhappy, constructs a personalized performance, creates a temporary world around that person, and photographs the result.

Her instinct is powerful, but she still tends to decide what another person needs before listening.

## Mai

Mai is beginning to reclaim Kumai from its origin in Yua. Game streaming could allow her to reveal a more integrated version of herself without abandoning the protective distance of the account.

## Azu

Azu now participates in ordinary group care:

-   accepts shared spending for working conditions;

-   buys Choco’s gift;

-   contributes aesthetic judgment;

-   and criticizes Yuina without threatening the group’s existence.


Her sharpness has become an established relational language rather than permanent hostility.

## Keroppe

Keroppe is comparatively peripheral, which itself demonstrates the group’s growth. The girls can now:

-   interview customers;

-   organize labor;

-   govern some resources;

-   design performances;

-   and create emotional experiences without his direction.


His complaint about being excluded from the photograph also suggests that he wants recognition as part of the group rather than solely its administrator.

---

# Cumulative reading after six episodes

The series has moved from forming a team to examining the roles that stabilize it.

-   Yuina supplies emotional momentum.

-   Azu supplies uncompromising judgment.

-   Kyouka supplies organization and mediation.

-   Choco supplies cheer and social softness.

-   Mai supplies practical systems awareness and understated competence.


Episode 6 asks what happens when those roles become prisons.

Kyouka does not want to be endlessly useful.

Choco may not want to be endlessly happy.

Mai no longer wants her work to belong spiritually to Yua.

Azu is learning that judgment need not be cruelty.

Yuina has not yet recognized that emotional momentum can also become pressure.

The working thesis can therefore be expanded:

> *Maebashi Witches* is not merely about girls learning to understand customers. It is about girls learning to stop treating one another as the convenient ensemble functions through which the group operates.\*

The birthday party proves that they care about Choco.

The coming question is whether they can understand her when she stops being “Choco-chan.”

---

## Clips that would most refine the analysis

The highest-value clips are:

1.  **00:00–01:18** — the cold-open customer performance. Choco’s delivery and the aggressively positive song are important for determining whether its tone is reassuring, deliberately shallow, or already faintly unstable.

2.  **08:36–11:37** — Yuina and Kyouka discussing why they want to become witches, followed by the Mogutan reveal. Kyouka’s pauses and vocal change between her public altruistic answer and private escape motive are central.

3.  **16:41–17:39** — Kyouka’s conversation with her mother and retreat into Mogutan’s stream. This would clarify whether Kyouka sounds resigned, irritated, emotionally absent, or practiced in placating her parents.

4.  **18:22–21:35** — Choco asking for Mapo, the surprise reveal, Yuina’s entire birthday song, Choco’s tears, and the repeated birthday greeting. This is the most important request because Choco’s emotional acting may substantially refine what the cakes mean to her.


A brief **23:09–23:31** clip of the attempted group photograph and customer interruption would also help determine exactly whose irritation closes the episode.

# *Maebashi Witches* Episode 7

## 「しんどい時しんどいって言うのしんどい」

### “When things are hard, it’s hard to say they’re hard”

## Bundle assessment

The Episode 7 bundle appears complete:

-   23:41 runtime

-   451 Japanese subtitle cues

-   595 retained frames after visual deduplication

-   30 contact sheets

-   complete cold open, ordinary customer follow-up, Choco’s hidden home life, ending, and post-credits sequence


This episode does not fully resolve the Choco–Kyouka conflict. Instead, it dismantles the assumptions on which that conflict rests and positions both girls for a more direct confrontation in Episode 8.

Its central question is:

> **What happens when a person becomes so identified with being useful, cheerful, or responsible that expressing distress feels like a betrayal of everyone who depends on her?**

---

# Episode summary

## Choco insults a customer

The episode begins immediately after Choco’s interrupted birthday photograph.

An elderly woman enters the flower shop, apparently confused about the girls’ identities. Choco, still angry that the customer disrupted her celebration, mutters:

> 「ふざけんな ババア」
> “You’ve got to be kidding, old hag.”

When confronted, she pretends that she merely said something resembling an enthusiastic crowd chant.

The woman nevertheless possesses a genuine wish, so the girls serve her and successfully bloom her heart-flower.

Afterward, Yuina wants to resume Choco’s birthday celebration. Kyouka refuses to ignore what happened. She lists Choco’s conduct:

-   trying to drive out a customer;

-   clicking her tongue;

-   verbally insulting the woman;

-   and then lying about it.


The others minimize the incident because the customer did not seem to hear, her wish was fulfilled, and she left happy.

Kyouka asks:

> 「今 この子のこと心からお祝いできる？」
> “Can you sincerely celebrate this girl right now?”

Choco responds by withdrawing from the party completely. She tells them they no longer need to celebrate her and accuses them of wearing expressions that say:

> “We went to all this effort for you, and this is how you act?”

She apologizes for wasting their preparation, promises to return as her normal “Choco-chan” the following day, and leaves.

The cakes remain uneaten in the shop refrigerator.

## Kyouka questions whether she is wrong

Kyouka asks whether she caused the disaster.

Keroppe tells her that extremely serious girls who never permit themselves mistakes are often the ones who abandon witch training.

He observes that Kyouka has never spent Mapo for her own pleasure and suggests she try it.

Kyouka refuses. Shared Mapo belongs to the whole group.

Keroppe partly admits that he wants her to remain because she matters to him, then confesses a more curious motive: he wants to see what Kyouka would choose if she acted entirely according to desire.

Kyouka goes home to complete her schoolwork instead.

## Kyouka’s parents discuss her future

At home, Kyouka listens from another room as her parents discuss moving.

Her mother believes Kyouka’s recent clothing, speech, phone use, and friendships indicate that her environment is corrupting her. She wants Kyouka placed in an environment appropriate to the family’s educational and social expectations.

Her father assumes that Kyouka will eventually acquire the necessary awareness as heir to the Kitahara family.

They do not discuss what Kyouka wants. They discuss the person she must become.

## Mogutan recognizes Kyouka

Kyouka retreats to Mogutan’s livestream.

Mogutan reads one of Kyouka’s messages and publicly thanks “Kyon-chan” for continuing to watch, tip, and attend events. Mogutan remembers her.

Kyouka is visibly thrilled.

The following day, the other witches find her unnervingly cheerful:

-   she skips up stairs;

-   hums while watering flowers;

-   and seems completely untroubled by the birthday failure.


The group initially assumes that Kyouka and Choco reconciled. They soon realize that Choco remains uncomfortable and has stopped using her affectionate “Kyoukarion” nickname.

The reconciliation exists only in Kyouka’s outward mood.

## Choco criticizes the elderly customer’s wish

Kyouka and Choco later see the elderly customer carrying out the wish granted at the shop.

The woman has temporarily regained enough ability to prepare dinner and welcome home her exhausted daughter:

> “You’ve worked hard. Dinner is ready, so go take a bath.”

Choco questions why she chose such a modest wish. In Choco’s view, the woman could have asked for something more consequential:

-   not to burden her family;

-   to return to a time before her cognitive confusion;

-   or to remove the underlying problem.


Kyouka answers that the witches do not need to judge what a customer chooses to want.

Choco then tells Kyouka that she prefers her usual angry expression. Kyouka’s excessive smiling and consideration make Choco uncomfortable.

She wants “Kyoukarion” to remain recognizably herself.

## Choco collapses from exhaustion

After plans for the upcoming Tanabata festival, Choco falls into a deep sleep.

Keroppe tells Yuina that Choco has been pushing herself too hard and allows Yuina to experience Choco’s ordinary life.

Time moves backward into daytime.

Choco remains asleep while Yuina is perceived by Choco’s family as Chiyoko herself.

Yuina is made to perform the labor Choco normally completes.

## Choco’s home life

Choco’s grandmother has a leg injury, has stopped regularly attending rehabilitation, and asks Choco to massage her legs.

Choco also cares for two younger siblings, Yayoi and Taizou:

-   collects them;

-   cooks for them;

-   feeds and holds them;

-   entertains them;

-   and supervises the household.


The family’s curry contains conspicuously large vegetables but almost no meat. Choco explains that the refrigerator is often nearly empty before her mother’s payday.

Her father died shortly after Taizou’s birth.

Her mother is a nurse who frequently works nights. Choco takes part-time shifts when her mother is home and able to supervise the family.

Her grandmother previously handled much of the domestic work, but after falling, breaking her leg, and resisting rehabilitation, she became another person requiring care.

Choco therefore juggles:

-   school;

-   witch training;

-   domestic labor;

-   sibling care;

-   elder care;

-   cooking;

-   shopping;

-   part-time employment;

-   and magical time reversal that allows her to fit more work into the same day.


Keroppe recruited her by promising:

> 「魔女になれば お金に不自由することはない」
> “If you become a witch, you’ll never have to worry about money.”

> 「なんでもできる」
> “You’ll be able to do anything.”

Choco wants to:

-   spare her younger siblings hardship;

-   make her mother’s life easier;

-   and restore her grandmother’s health.


She then asks whether Yuina is disgusted or put off by what she has seen.

## Choco asks for secrecy

Afterward, Choco asks Yuina not to tell the other girls.

She wants to remain “Choco-chan” in front of them. She does not want to be seen as pitiable.

Yuina agrees immediately:

> Friends do not need to disclose everything to one another.

> Choco can decide what she does and does not show.

Yuina then observes that Choco’s family clearly loves her. Everyone smiles when they sees her because Choco is always performing the smiling, hardworking Choco-chan.

Yuina tentatively asks for one thing:

> When Choco is suffering, she wants Choco to say that she is suffering.

Choco points out the contradiction embodied in the episode title:

> Saying that things are hard is itself hard.

It means risking other people’s judgment, pity, discomfort, and possible disappointment.

Choco argues that many families have more severe difficulties. Her mother encounters such cases through nursing work. Choco can still attend high school, so she believes she has no right to complain.

Yuina rejects the comparison:

> 「他は関係ないの」
> “Other people aren’t relevant.”

> 「うちはチョコちゃんがしんどいかどうかの話をしてるの」
> “We’re talking about whether things are hard for Choco.”

Yuina says that outsiders often cannot tell when a hardworking person is suffering. At minimum, she wants Choco to tell the people around her because knowing may allow something to change.

Later, Yuina asks Keroppe whether she sounded self-righteous.

Keroppe answers:

> Choco is the one who gets to decide that.

## Post-credits: Mogutan’s private message

Mogutan publicly tells Kyouka not to overextend herself:

> She does not need to send so much money.

> Simply visiting the stream is enough.

Kyouka is deeply moved.

The public reassurance is followed by private direct messages.

Mogutan:

-   says Kyouka gives them strength;

-   remembers her from an in-person event;

-   calls her a good and beautiful girl;

-   notes that she lives outside Tokyo;

-   suggests meeting privately;

-   offers to show her around Tokyo;

-   mentions preparing to shower;

-   and asks Kyouka to send a photograph.


Kyouka reacts with breathless excitement rather than alarm.

The episode ends on the next title:

> 「キョウカちゃんって馬鹿なんだね」
> “Kyouka-chan, you really are stupid.”

---

# Central thesis

Episode 7 is about **the social cost of admitting need**.

Choco does not merely conceal that she is busy. She has built an identity designed to make her burden invisible:

-   she turns statements into cute slogans;

-   converts fatigue into jokes;

-   arrives as “Choco-chan” rather than Chiyoko;

-   eats, sleeps, and acts frivolously in public;

-   and allows others to think she is irresponsible rather than permit them to pity her.


Kyouka performs the opposite role:

-   disciplined;

-   useful;

-   morally correct;

-   self-controlled;

-   and incapable of tolerating visible disorder.


Both girls conceal need through performance.

> **Choco disguises suffering as carelessness.**

> **Kyouka disguises captivity as competence.**

---

# The birthday party becomes conditional care

In Episode 6, the birthday appeared to prove that the others loved Choco as an individual.

Episode 7 immediately complicates that conclusion.

The celebration survives only as long as Choco performs a version of herself that the others feel comfortable loving.

Once she acts cruelly, Kyouka asks whether they can sincerely celebrate her.

Kyouka is not wrong to hold her accountable. Choco’s conduct toward the customer is unacceptable, and the others’ attempt to excuse it because no harm was visibly registered is ethically weak.

But Kyouka’s question makes the celebration feel conditional:

> Are you currently good enough to deserve the care we prepared?

Choco is acutely sensitive to that implication. She refuses to eat a cake while everyone is silently reassessing whether she merits it.

This helps explain why her joy in Episode 6 was so intense. She believed the cakes were **entirely hers**.

Episode 7 transforms them into gifts accompanied by judgment.

The uneaten cakes become suspended affection: real care that neither side can presently receive without resentment.

---

# Choco’s cruelty is not excused by exhaustion

The episode gives Choco an enormously sympathetic context, but it does not retroactively make insulting the elderly customer acceptable.

That distinction is important.

Choco was:

-   exhausted;

-   emotionally interrupted;

-   accustomed to caring for elderly and dependent relatives;

-   and finally receiving a rare moment centered on herself.


The customer’s arrival took that moment away.

Choco’s hostility is understandable as displaced resentment:

> Another older person has arrived needing something from her at the precise moment she was permitted to receive.

But the customer is not responsible for Choco’s home situation.

The series continues its pattern of separating explanation from absolution.

---

# The elderly customer mirrors Choco

Choco considers the customer’s wish wasteful.

The woman could have asked for health, cognitive clarity, independence, or relief from being a burden. Instead, she asks for a temporary opportunity to make dinner and care for her daughter.

That choice is not irrational. The woman wants to recover a meaningful role:

> not merely to require care, but to give it.

Choco struggles to recognize this because caregiving is already the organizing condition of her life. To her, making dinner for an exhausted family member is not a magical wish. It is the minimum duty she performs every day.

Yet the woman’s wish reveals something important:

> Care can be burdensome labor and still be a source of identity, dignity, and love.

That is also true for Choco.

She does not simply want the witches to remove her family from her life or release her from every obligation. She wants enough power, money, energy, and time to care for them successfully.

Her problem is not that she loves caring.

It is that the entire system depends upon her caring without limit.

---

# Time reversal as invisible labor

Choco uses magic not to escape responsibility but to create more hours in which she can perform it.

That is one of the show’s most pointed uses of supernatural power so far.

A magical-girl transformation usually expands what a heroine can accomplish. Choco’s magic expands the amount of labor others can extract from her life.

She repeatedly revisits the day to fit in:

-   domestic work;

-   employment;

-   school;

-   witch training;

-   and family care.


The cost appears as chronic exhaustion. The others interpret that exhaustion as laziness.

This is an elegant metaphor for invisible labor:

> The work happens in time no one else sees, so the tired worker appears as though she has done nothing.

Choco literally borrows time from herself.

---

# Choco is parentified, but her family is not villainous

The household burden is severe, yet the episode avoids inventing a single cruel family member who can be blamed.

Her mother works demanding nursing shifts.

Her father is dead.

Her siblings are young.

Her grandmother is injured, discouraged, and dependent.

The family genuinely loves Choco. Yuina correctly notices that everyone brightens when she appears.

That love does not make the arrangement sustainable.

The episode’s social diagnosis is structural:

-   bereavement;

-   limited income;

-   insufficient care support;

-   irregular medical work;

-   elder disability;

-   childcare;

-   and a teenage girl quietly absorbing the gaps.


Choco’s suffering is produced by people needing one another more than the household’s resources can support.

---

# “Other people have it worse”

Choco uses comparative suffering to revoke her own right to speak.

She can attend high school. Other families experience more severe hardship. Her mother tells stories from hospital work. Therefore, Choco concludes that calling her own life difficult would be indulgent.

Yuina’s answer is one of her strongest moral insights so far:

> Other suffering does not determine whether Choco is suffering.

This refuses a common hierarchy of legitimate pain.

A person does not need to prove that her hardship is the greatest available hardship before asking for help.

---

# Yuina learns to respect privacy

Yuina’s response represents meaningful growth.

Earlier episodes repeatedly showed her:

-   intruding into other people’s emotional lives;

-   assuming what would motivate them;

-   turning moments into photographs;

-   and pursuing friendship after explicit rejection.


Here, she tells Choco:

> Friends do not need complete access to one another.

> Choco decides what remains private.

Yuina does not immediately summon the other witches or insist that openness is always healthier.

She makes a narrower request: when the burden becomes unbearable, tell someone.

This is a much more mature model of care:

> privacy without total isolation.

---

# “Saying it is hard is hard”

The episode title is intentionally repetitive because it enacts the problem linguistically.

To say **しんどい** requires Choco to perform several difficult actions:

-   recognize that the burden exceeds her capacity;

-   stop comparing herself to worse cases;

-   risk being pitied;

-   risk making her mother or grandmother feel guilty;

-   admit that “Choco-chan” cannot keep everyone happy;

-   and create a problem that other people may then feel obligated to solve.


The request for help is itself additional emotional labor.

That is why Yuina cannot simply command her to speak.

Yuina acknowledges:

> 「しんどいときしんどいって言うの しんどいよね」
> “When things are hard, saying they’re hard is hard, isn’t it?”

She does not pretend disclosure is effortless.

---

# Choco wants to remain “Choco-chan”

Choco’s exaggerated speaking style is now clearly more than a comedic gimmick.

“Choco-chan” is:

-   a public persona;

-   a family mood-regulation strategy;

-   a way to transform fatigue into cuteness;

-   protection against pity;

-   and a self she genuinely enjoys inhabiting.


The persona is not fake in the simple sense.

Choco really is playful, affectionate, food-loving, and theatrically cute.

But the persona has become compulsory. She believes that showing any other state would threaten the people who depend on her smile.

This continues the series’ refusal to divide selves into “real” and “false”:

> Choco-chan is real.

> The suffering Chiyoko is also real.

The problem is that she permits the group to see only one of them.

---

# Kyouka’s moral rigidity

Kyouka is justified in refusing to celebrate cruelty as though nothing happened.

But her insistence on moral consistency is connected to the system under which she lives.

At home, Kyouka must be:

-   academically excellent;

-   socially appropriate;

-   feminine in the approved manner;

-   useful to the family;

-   and worthy of becoming its heir.


She responds by constructing an equally exacting personal ethic.

Shared Mapo must never be used selfishly.

Wrongdoing must be named.

An apology cannot be replaced with cheerful avoidance.

A customer’s wish must not be judged.

Kyouka’s values are often correct. The danger is that she treats correctness as the condition under which relationships remain safe.

Keroppe recognizes that this kind of person eventually collapses.

---

# Keroppe’s invitation to selfishness

Keroppe tells Kyouka that she has never used Mapo for herself and asks what she would do if she acted according to pure desire.

His intervention is partly manipulative curiosity, but it also identifies a genuine problem:

> Kyouka does not know how to want something without first making it socially useful.

Her dream of helping others doubles as permission to escape home.

Her leadership at the shop doubles as proof that she deserves autonomy.

Even her fandom initially appears modest and responsible: small tips, quiet viewing, private relaxation.

Keroppe is asking whether Kyouka possesses a desire that does not require moral justification.

Mogutan appears to offer exactly that desire.

---

# Kyouka’s family and Choco’s family

The episode places two sharply different households beside each other.

## Kyouka’s home

-   materially wealthy;

-   spacious;

-   educationally privileged;

-   socially prestigious;

-   and emotionally scripted.


Kyouka’s parents discuss her as an heir and product of environment. Their concern is not whether she is happy, but whether she is developing correctly.

## Choco’s home

-   economically strained;

-   crowded with care needs;

-   dependent upon irregular labor;

-   emotionally warm;

-   and physically exhausting.


Choco is needed too much.

Kyouka is defined too much.

One girl lacks practical freedom because her household cannot function without her.

The other lacks psychological freedom because her family has already authored her future.

Both are recruited by magic through a promise of escape.

---

# Mogutan gives Kyouka what her family withholds

Mogutan remembers Kyouka’s chosen name:

> Kyon-chan.

Mogutan thanks her personally, remembers seeing her, and tells her that her presence matters.

This is almost perfectly tailored to Kyouka’s emotional deprivation.

Her parents praise:

-   test scores;

-   inherited beauty;

-   family suitability;

-   and her usefulness as a daughter.


Mogutan appears to praise Kyouka simply for being there.

That is why her mood changes so dramatically.

She feels individually recognized rather than institutionally evaluated.

The other witches interpret her happiness as reconciliation because they cannot see the private source of validation.

---

# The false reconciliation

Kyouka becomes cheerful immediately after Mogutan acknowledges her.

Choco remains visibly uncomfortable.

The others initially conclude that the conflict has somehow resolved because Kyouka looks happy.

This repeats one of the series’ central mistakes:

> treating visible affect as reliable evidence of internal resolution.

Kyouka’s smile does not mean she forgave Choco or reconsidered her judgment.

Her attention has been emotionally displaced elsewhere.

Choco recognizes this more accurately than the others. She tells Kyouka that her forced cheer and consideration feel wrong. She prefers Kyouka’s familiar irritation.

Choco, who specializes in performing false ease, can identify it in someone else.

---

# Choco and Kyouka as mirrors

The episode prepares them as one of the show’s most productive pairings.

Each can perceive the other’s performance while remaining trapped inside her own.

---

# The post-credits sequence

The final scene shifts Mogutan from comforting parasocial figure to a serious danger.

Publicly, Mogutan tells Kyouka:

-   not to overdo herself;

-   not to spend too much;

-   and that simply watching is enough.


Privately, Mogutan:

-   emphasizes remembering her physical appearance;

-   calls her beautiful;

-   identifies that she is a regional teenage fan;

-   proposes a private meeting in Tokyo;

-   introduces intimate shower imagery;

-   and requests a photograph.


At minimum, this is a severe breach of creator–fan boundaries.

Given Kyouka’s age and vulnerability, it also carries clear grooming implications.

The sequence does not yet prove everything about Mogutan’s offline identity or ultimate intent. But the progression is recognizable:

1.  Publicly present a safe, affirming persona.

2.  Reward a highly invested fan with personal recognition.

3.  Move communication into private messages.

4.  Compliment appearance and specialness.

5.  suggest an exclusive meeting.

6.  introduce sexual or intimate context.

7.  request personal material.


Kyouka is especially vulnerable because she interprets private recognition as freedom from being used.

She may instead be entering another relationship in which her usefulness—money, attention, beauty, and devotion—is being cultivated.

---

# The public and private Mogutan

Mogutan also mirrors the witches’ recurring identity problem.

The public Mogutan says:

> Be yourself. Relax. Do not overextend yourself.

The private Mogutan displays:

> 「欲望全開」
> “desire at full throttle,”

as Keroppe observes.

The comforting public persona may not be wholly false. A creator can sincerely care about viewers while also exploiting a particular fan.

But the discrepancy is central.

Kyouka believes Mogutan is a space free from imposed roles. The private message begins assigning her a new one:

> beautiful, devoted, available fan.

---

# Character updates

## Choco

Episode 7 completely reframes her apparent laziness.

She is not conserving effort because she lacks commitment. She is depleted by labor no one sees.

Her central defense is to make the burden look unserious:

-   sleep becomes a Choco-chan trait;

-   lateness becomes comedy;

-   hunger becomes a catchphrase;

-   suffering becomes invisible;

-   and cruelty is disguised as another joke.


Her arc will require something more difficult than accepting help. She must learn that receiving care does not automatically convert her into an object of pity.

## Kyouka

Kyouka’s correctness is both moral strength and survival strategy.

She wants people to act sincerely because her own life is saturated with polite scripts.

Her anger at Choco partly arises because Choco’s behavior threatens the order through which Kyouka maintains control.

Mogutan’s recognition gives her an intoxicating alternative identity, but the post-credits scene suggests she is transferring her unmet need for unconditional recognition into an unsafe relationship.

## Yuina

Yuina displays significant emotional growth.

She:

-   listens without immediately fixing;

-   honors confidentiality;

-   refuses comparative suffering;

-   recognizes invisible labor;

-   admits that her advice may sound self-important;

-   and leaves moral evaluation to Choco.


Her language remains repetitive and improvised, but that awkwardness makes her care less like a prepared therapeutic doctrine and more like genuine attention.

## Keroppe

Keroppe continues recruiting girls through promises precisely tailored to their vulnerabilities:

-   Azu: control of embodiment.

-   Mai: relational proximity.

-   Choco: freedom from poverty and practical limitation.

-   Kyouka: possibly freedom through socially useful power.


He understands their hidden problems better than the girls do, but regularly allows those problems to intensify.

His exposure of Choco’s private life to Yuina also continues the series’ unresolved concern about consent, even if Choco later accepts Yuina’s knowledge.

---

# Cumulative reading after seven episodes

The first customer stories taught the witches not to substitute their own assumptions for another person’s wish.

The series is now turning the same scrutiny inward.

The girls have assigned each other stable ensemble roles:

-   Yuina is emotional enthusiasm.

-   Azu is blunt judgment.

-   Mai is detached practicality.

-   Kyouka is responsible competence.

-   Choco is uncomplicated cheer.


Episode 7 demonstrates that these roles are not merely incomplete. They conceal the exact forms of suffering the group most needs to understand.

The larger thesis can now be refined:

> *Maebashi Witches* is about girls learning that making another person’s heart bloom requires more than producing happiness. It requires creating conditions in which unhappiness can be spoken without the speaker losing dignity, usefulness, privacy, or belonging.\*

Choco’s birthday proves the group can make her happy.

Episode 7 asks whether they can tolerate her when she is angry, exhausted, unfair, needy, and no longer entertaining.

Kyouka’s story asks the inverse:

> Can Kyouka believe she deserves freedom before proving herself endlessly useful—and can she distinguish genuine recognition from someone strategically making her feel special?

I’ll hold the final clip prioritization until after examining Episode 8, so the requested scenes can be selected across the complete Choco–Kyouka arc rather than episode by episode.
